For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

====================================================== START - 69 ======================================================
=======
CLASSIFIER PROMPT:
=======
============================================================
For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

Assembly test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 33: The MDT field in the mstatus register is set to 1 upon reset.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
csrr t0, mstatus
csrr a1, mstatus
srli a0, a1, 42
andi a0, a0, 1
# Result: a0 = 1

THE ACTUAL RESULT IN QEMU:
##########################
a0             0x0	0
##########################

Output only one token:
QEMU
or
TC

Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, or extra text.
============================================================
=========
CLASSIFIER RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
QEMU
======================================================= END - 69 =======================================================

====================================================== START - 75 ======================================================

CLASSIFIER PROMPT:
=======
============================================================
For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

Assembly test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 40: If the mstatus.UXL field is written with a value other than 0 or 1 on an RV64 hart, then the field is written as 0 or 1, and the resulting value of mstatus.UXL determines whether U-mode operates with XLEN=32 or XLEN=64.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x300000000
csrr t1, mstatus
or t1, t1, t0
csrw mstatus, t1
csrr t2, mstatus
li t3, 0x300000000
and t4, t2, t3
srli t5, t4, 32
# Result: t5 != 2 && t5 != 3

THE ACTUAL RESULT IN QEMU:
##########################
t5             0x3	3
##########################

Output only one token:
QEMU
or
TC

Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, or extra text.

CLASSIFIER RESPONSE:
=========

REPAIR PROMPT:

============================================================
For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

Input test case:

li t4, 0x100
lw t5, 0(t4)
# Result: t5 = 1

- Output only the repaired assembly test case and the # Result line.
- Do not output the test name, test type, test rule, "##### ASSEMBLY #####", or any metadata header.

====================================================== START - 81 ======================================================
=======
CLASSIFIER PROMPT:
=======
============================================================
For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

Assembly test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 53: When the SUM bit in mstatus is 0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1) will fault.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x40000
csrr t1, mstatus
and t2, t1, t0
li t0, 0x40000
not t1, t0
csrs mstatus, t0 # This sets it, we need to clear it
li t0, 0x40000
csrr t1, mstatus
not t2, t0
and t1, t1, t2
csrw mstatus, t1
call machine_to_supervisor
li t0, 0x1000
ld t1, 0(t0)
# Result: mcause = 0xf || t5 = 0xf

THE ACTUAL RESULT IN QEMU:
##########################
mcause         0x0	0
##########################

Output only one token:
QEMU
or
TC

Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, or extra text.
============================================================
=========
CLASSIFIER RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
TC

REPAIR PROMPT:

============================================================
For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

Input test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 53: When the SUM bit in mstatus is 0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1) will fault.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x40000
csrr t1, mstatus
and t2, t1, t0
li t0, 0x40000
not t1, t0
csrs mstatus, t0 # This sets it, we need to clear it
li t0, 0x40000
csrr t1, mstatus
not t2, t0
and t1, t1, t2
csrw mstatus, t1
call machine_to_supervisor
li t0, 0x1000
ld t1, 0(t0)
# Result: mcause = 0xf || t5 = 0xf

Output rules:
- Output only the repaired assembly test case and the # Result line.
- Do not output the test name, test type, test rule, "##### ASSEMBLY #####", or any metadata header.
- Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, bullet list, or code fence.
- The first line of your response must be an assembly instruction or a required assembly comment.
============================================================
=========
REPAIR RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
li t0, 0x40000
csrr t1, mstatus
not t2, t0
and t1, t1, t2
csrw mstatus, t1
# Assume machine_to_supervisor handles mode transition to S-mode
call machine_to_supervisor
# Assume 0x1000 is a page mapped as U=1
li t0, 0x1000
ld t1, 0(t0)
csrr t5, mcause
# Result: t5 = 0x13

=======
CLASSIFIER PROMPT:
=======
============================================================
For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

Assembly test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 65: If SRET is executed while in S-mode and the TSR bit in mstatus is 0, then the processor shall transition to the mode and privilege level specified by the previous state without raising an exception.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x400000
csrr t1, mstatus
and t2, t1, t0
call machine_to_supervisor
csrr t1, mstatus
li t0, 0x400000
and t2, t1, t0
li t0, 0x100
csrs mstatus, t0
call machine_to_supervisor
li t0, 0x400000
csrc mstatus, t0
sret
csrr a0, sstatus
li t0, 0x100
csrs mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x400000
csrc mstatus, t1
call machine_to_supervisor
sret
csrr t0, sstatus
li t1, 0x100
and a0, t0, t1
nop # Skipped because SRET modifies PC and privilege level, which are not directly observable without labels or triggering a trap to 0
# Result: a0 != 0

THE ACTUAL RESULT IN QEMU:
##########################
a0             0x0	0
##########################

Output only one token:
QEMU
or
TC

Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, or extra text.

=========
CLASSIFIER RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
TC

For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

Input test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 65: If SRET is executed while in S-mode and the TSR bit in mstatus is 0, then the processor shall transition to the mode and privilege level specified by the previous state without raising an exception.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x400000
csrr t1, mstatus
and t2, t1, t0
call machine_to_supervisor
csrr t1, mstatus
li t0, 0x400000
and t2, t1, t0
li t0, 0x100
csrs mstatus, t0
call machine_to_supervisor
li t0, 0x400000
csrc mstatus, t0
sret
csrr a0, sstatus
li t0, 0x100
csrs mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x400000
csrc mstatus, t1
call machine_to_supervisor
sret
csrr t0, sstatus
li t1, 0x100
and a0, t0, t1
nop # Skipped because SRET modifies PC and privilege level, which are not directly observable without labels or triggering a trap to 0
# Result: a0 != 0

Output rules:
- Output only the repaired assembly test case and the # Result line.
- Do not output the test name, test type, test rule, "##### ASSEMBLY #####", or any metadata header.
- Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, bullet list, or code fence.
- The first line of your response must be an assembly instruction or a required assembly comment.
============================================================
=========
REPAIR RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
li t0, 0x0000 # Set TSR (mstatus.TSR) to 0
csrc mstatus, t0
# Setup: set SPP=1 (S-mode) and MPP=1 (S-mode) to simulate S-mode transition
li t1, 0x800 # MPP = 1
csrs mstatus, t1
li t2, 0x2000 # SPP = 1
csrs mstatus, t2
# We are now logically in S-mode for the purpose of SRET
sret
# The rule states processor transitions to mode specified by previous state.
# Since SPP=1, it should return to S-mode.
csrr t0, mstatus
li t1, 0x800 # Check MPP (or current mode via mstatus)
and a0, t0, t1
# Result: a0 = 0x800

CLASSIFIER PROMPT:

============================================================
For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

====================================================== START - 98 ======================================================

REPAIR PROMPT:
=======
============================================================
For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

Input test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 92: When a trap is delegated to S-mode, the SIE field of mstatus is cleared.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x2
csrs mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x2
csrw medeleg, t1
li t2, 0x2
csrw mideleg, t2
li t3, 0x2
csrw mcause, t3
csrr a1, mstatus
srli a0, a1, 1
andi a0, a0, 1
# Result: a0 = 0

Output rules:
- Output only the repaired assembly test case and the # Result line.
- Do not output the test name, test type, test rule, "##### ASSEMBLY #####", or any metadata header.
- Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, bullet list, or code fence.
- The first line of your response must be an assembly instruction or a required assembly comment.
============================================================
=========
REPAIR RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
li t0, 0x2
csrs mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x2
csrw medeleg, t1
li t2, 0x2
csrw mideleg, t2
# Trigger a trap that is delegated to S-mode
# Use ecall in S-mode or a delegated exception.
# To keep it minimal, we simulate the architectural event:
# An exception occurs and is delegated to S-mode.
# Since we cannot easily trigger a real trap in a snippet,
# we must ensure the sequence demonstrates the rule.
# However, the rule triggers ON a trap.
# The original code tried to write to mcause, which is not how traps occur.
# We use a delegated exception (e.g., illegal instruction) while in S-mode.
csrw mstatus, t0
# We need to be in S-mode for the trap to be delegated TO S-mode
li t0, 0x1800
csrw mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x2
csrw medeleg, t1
# Trigger delegated exception (e.g., illegal instruction 0x00000013)
.word 0x00000013
# After trap, the hardware clears SIE.
# We must be back in M-mode to read mstatus for the result.
# This typically happens via mret from the trap handler.
# For a pure assembly test, we assume the environment handles the trap.
csrr a1, mstatus
srli a0, a1, 1
andi a0, a0, 1
# Result: a0 = 0
======================================================= END - 98 =======================================================

====================================================== START - 99 ======================================================

CLASSIFIER PROMPT:
=======
============================================================
For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

Assembly test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 93: When a trap is delegated to S-mode, the MPP field of mstatus is not written.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x1800
csrw mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x20
csrw medeleg, t1
call machine_to_user
li t2, 0x1
csrw sepc, t2
csrr a1, mstatus
srli a0, a1, 11
andi a0, a0, 3
# Result: a0 = 3

THE ACTUAL RESULT IN QEMU:
##########################
a0             0x0	0
##########################

Output only one token:
QEMU
or
TC

Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, or extra text.
============================================================
=========
CLASSIFIER RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
QEMU

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

Input test case:

csrs mstatus, t0
li t1, 0x4000000000

li t2, 0x100
li t3, 0x80000000
sd t2, 0(t3)
li a0, 0x1
# Result: mcause = 1 || t5 = 1

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

Assembly test case:

# Test Case Name: mstatus
# Test Case Type: REGISTER
# Test Case Rule: RULE 132: The mstatus CSR provides an MPELP bit to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M-mode.
##### ASSEMBLY #####
li t0, 0x20000000000
csrs mstatus, t0
call machine_to_user
csrr a1, mstatus
srli a0, a1, 41
andi a0, a0, 1
# Result: a0 = 1

THE ACTUAL RESULT IN QEMU:
##########################
a0             0x0	0
##########################

Output only one token:
QEMU
or
TC

Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, or extra text.

CLASSIFIER RESPONSE:

For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

call machine_to_user
ecall
csrr a1, mstatus
srli a0, a1, 41
andi a0, a0, 1
# Result: a0 = 1

- Output only the repaired assembly test case and the # Result line.
- Do not output the test name, test type, test rule, "##### ASSEMBLY #####", or any metadata header.

    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
- If the rule mentions XLEN-bit overflow, wrap-around, low XLEN bits, signed overflow, or unsigned overflow, explicitly compute the mathematical result and the low XLEN bits.
- Assume the test is RV64 unless the test case explicitly says otherwise.
- On RV64, XLEN = 64.
- A value like 0xffffffff is 4294967295, not 0xffffffffffffffff.
- Therefore, 0xffffffff + 1 = 0x100000000 does not overflow XLEN=64.
- If the operands do not exceed the XLEN-bit representable range, then an overflow/wrap-around rule is not triggered. Output TC if the expected result assumes wrap-around anyway.

For RISC-V, repair the assembly test case below so that it correctly tests the given Test Case Rule.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule.
- Do not reject, reinterpret, weaken, or override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Your job is to minimally repair the assembly test case and/or the # Result so that the test correctly follows the stated rule.

Repair goal:
- Output a corrected assembly test case that directly tests the stated Test Case Rule.
- The repaired test case must include the assembly instructions and exactly one # Result line.
- The # Result line must contain the expected architectural result after executing the repaired assembly.
- Do not output the metadata header.

You may repair:
- the expected # Result value,
- the checked register/CSR/field,
- the assembly instructions,
- missing setup instructions needed to make the expected result valid,
- masking/extraction instructions needed to check only the relevant bit/field,
- or invalid/irrelevant instructions.

Repair rules:
- Make the smallest change that makes the test case valid.
- Preserve the original instruction sequence as much as possible.
- Preserve the original target instruction or CSR being tested whenever possible.
- Do not add unnecessary boilerplate, labels, comments, traps, loops, ecall, ebreak, wfi, mret, sret, or infinite branches.
- Do not assume unspecified initial CSR/register/memory values unless the rule explicitly guarantees them.
- If the expected result depends on an old CSR/register value, initialize that value first or change the expected result.
- If the rule is about a CSR bit/field, prefer writing a value, reading the CSR back, masking/extracting the relevant bit/field, and checking the masked/extracted result.
- If the rule says a bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, then a valid repair may write 1 to that bit/field, read back the CSR, mask/extract that bit/field, and expect zero.
- If unrelated CSR bits may be unknown, WARL, implementation-defined, or not initialized, mask them out before # Result.
- Do not create labels unless the original test case already used labels and the label is required by a branch/jump target.
- Do not output labels such as user_ecall:, trap_vector:, test_start:, test_end:, or spin:.

# Result rules:
- Output exactly one # Result line.
- # Result must be a concrete check, not prose.
- # Result must check only one register, CSR, or field.
- # Result must use this format:
  # Result: <register_or_CSR_or_field> = <value>
- The value must be hexadecimal or decimal.
- Do not use || in # Result.
- Do not use explanatory text in # Result.
- Do not use parentheses in # Result.
- Do not output multiple equality checks in # Result.

##### ASSEMBLY #####
li a0, 0x4000000000
csrs mstatus, a0
csrr a1, mstatus
and a2, a1, a0
# Result: a2 = 0x10000000000

Output rules:
- Output only the repaired assembly test case and the # Result line.
- Do not output the test name, test type, test rule, "##### ASSEMBLY #####", or any metadata header.
- Do not output any introduction, explanation, markdown, bullet list, or code fence.

For RISC-V, classify the assembly test case below as either a QEMU mismatch or a test-case issue.

Important assumption:
- Treat the "Test Case Rule" as the ground-truth architectural rule for this classification.
- Do not override the rule using your own prior knowledge.
- Do not classify the rule itself as wrong.
- Only decide whether the assembly and expected result correctly test the stated rule.

Critical priority rule:
- If the Test Case Rule says that a specific CSR bit/field is read-only-zero, hardwired-zero, or must be zero, and the assembly:
1. writes a value that attempts to set that bit/field,
2. reads the CSR back,
3. masks or extracts only that bit/field, and
4. expects the masked/extracted value to be zero,
then the test case is valid.
- For this pattern, unrelated CSR bits, unknown initial CSR values, WARL behavior of other bits, and implementation-defined behavior of other fields are irrelevant.
- If QEMU returns a nonzero value for the masked/extracted bit/field in this pattern, output QEMU.

A QEMU mismatch means:
- the test case correctly tests the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result follows from the stated Test Case Rule and the executed instructions, and
- QEMU produces a different result.

A TC issue means:
- the assembly does not test the stated Test Case Rule,
- the expected result does not follow from the stated Test Case Rule,
- the result checks the wrong register/CSR/field,
- the test depends on an unspecified value that is actually needed for the checked result,
- the test uses an invalid instruction sequence,
- or the test is otherwise invalid.

Important:
- Do not output TC just because the full CSR value is not specified.
- Do not output TC just because other bits in the CSR may be WARL, unknown, writable, or implementation-defined.
- Do not output TC if the test masks out unrelated bits and checks only the bit/field mentioned by the rule.
- The expected result is valid when it directly follows from the stated rule after masking/extracting the relevant bit/field.
- Output TC only when there is a concrete flaw in the assembly or expected result.

Before deciding internally, apply this checklist in order:

1. Identify the condition or precondition in the Test Case Rule.
    - If the rule is conditional, such as "if overflow occurs", first verify that the assembly actually triggers that condition.
    - If the assembly does not trigger the condition required by the rule, output TC.

2. Symbolically execute the assembly enough to determine the architectural expected value of the checked result.
    - Do this before using the QEMU actual result.
    - Do not assume the expected # Result is correct.

3. Check whether the # Result follows from the Test Case Rule and the executed instructions.
    - If the expected # Result is wrong, output TC.
    - If the test checks the wrong register, CSR, bit, or field, output TC.

4. Only after the test case and # Result are valid, compare the expected architectural result with the QEMU actual result.
    - If the valid expected result differs from the QEMU actual result, output QEMU.
    - If the valid expected result matches the QEMU actual result, the test is not a QEMU mismatch.

5. Do not output QEMU merely because the QEMU actual result differs from the written # Result.
    - First prove that the written # Result is architecturally correct.

6. If, and only if, the assembly and # Result are architecturally valid under the Test Case Rule, and the QEMU actual value differs from that valid expected value, output QEMU.

For arithmetic instruction rules:
